\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right]

\displaystyle=2\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in e\\
w\notin F\end{subarray}}|J_{wv}|+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in d\\
w\notin F\end{subarray}}|J_{uw}|+|h_{u}+h_{v}|+\sum_{f\in F}|J_{uf}+J_{vf}|\right),

(68)

\displaystyle\omega_{\max}\left[\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.54 0 C 9.54 5.27 5.27 9.54 0 9.54 C -5.27 9.54 -9.54 5.27 -9.54 0 C -9.54 -5.27 -5.27 -9.54 0 -9.54 C 5.27 -9.54 9.54 -5.27 9.54 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -3.46 -4.46)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\times\hbox to14.18pt{\vbox to14.18pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.09111pt\lower-7.09111pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.54 0 C 9.54 5.27 5.27 9.54 0 9.54 C -5.27 9.54 -9.54 5.27 -9.54 0 C -9.54 -5.27 -5.27 -9.54 0 -9.54 C 5.27 -9.54 9.54 -5.27 9.54 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -3.46 -4.46)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right]

\displaystyle=2\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in e\\
w\notin F\end{subarray}}|J_{wv}|+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in d\\
w\notin F\end{subarray}}|J_{uw}|+|h_{u}-h_{v}|+\sum_{f\in F}|J_{uf}-J_{vf}|\right).

(69)

Thus, the maximum frequencies of
f_{uv}(\mathbf{h},\gamma)
and
g_{uv}(\mathbf{h},\gamma)
are:

\displaystyle\omega_{\max}\left[f_{uv}(\mathbf{J},\mathbf{h},\gamma)\right]

\displaystyle=2\left(|J_{uv}|+\max\left\{|h_{v}|+\sum_{w\in e}|J_{wv}|,|h_{u}|+\sum_{w\in d}|J_{uw}|\right\}\right),

(70)

\displaystyle\omega_{\max}\left[g_{uv}(\mathbf{J},\mathbf{h},\gamma)\right]\!

\displaystyle=\!2\!\left(\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in e\\
w\notin F\end{subarray}}\!|J_{wv}|\!+\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in d\\
w\notin F\end{subarray}}\!|J_{uw}|\!+\!\max\!\left\{\!|h_{u}\!\pm\!h_{v}|\!+\!\sum_{f\in F}\!|J_{uf}\!\pm\!J_{vf}|\!\right\}\!\right).

(71)

Therefore, the maximum angular frequency of
\left\langle C_{uv}\right\rangle_{\gamma}
is the maximum of these two frequencies.
∎

Appendix D
Upper Bounds on the Sampling Periods

In this section, we establish upper bounds on the sampling period necessary to reconstruct the optimisation landscape of QAOA
1
for the SK model,
D
-regular triangle-free graphs, and triangle-free graphs with bounded maximum degrees. These bounds are proven by integrating
theorem

4
and
theorem

2
and leveraging the specific properties of each case.

D.1
Sherrington-Kirkpatrick Model

Proof of
Corollary

3
.

Consider the case without external fields. The maximum angular frequency of the
\gamma
terms in the expectation value is

\omega_{\max}\!\left[\!\left\langle C_{uv}\right\rangle_{\gamma}\right]\!=\!2\times\max\!\left\{\!|J_{uv}|\!+\!\max\!\left\{\!\sum_{w\in e}\!|J_{wv}|,\!\sum_{w\in d}\!|J_{uw}|\!\right\}\!,\!\max\!\left\{\!\sum_{f\in F}\!|J_{uf}\!\pm\!J_{vf}|\!\right\}\!\right\}.

(72)

Since the graph is complete, the number of neighbours excluding vertices
u
and
v
is
|e|=|d|=n-2
, and the number of triangles is
|F|=n-2
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\max\left\{\sum_{w\in e}|J_{wv}|,\sum_{w\in d}|J_{uw}|\right\}

\displaystyle=n-2,

(73)

\displaystyle\max\left\{\sum_{f\in F}|J_{uf}\pm J_{vf}|\right\}

\displaystyle=2n-4.

(74)

The maximum in the summation of the triangle terms in
eq.

74
is achieved when all edges forming a triangle with edge
\{u,v\}
have the same weights. Specifically, this occurs when
\operatorname{sgn}(J_{uf})=-\operatorname{sgn}(J_{vf})
for all
f\in F
. Under this condition, the maximum angular frequency of the
\gamma
terms scales as
\mathcal{O}(n)
.

This scaling remains valid for models with external fields of strength
\pm 1
, where the maximum angular frequency may differ by a constant factor compared to the case without external fields. Consequently, the maximum frequency is given by

\nu_{\max}=\frac{\mathcal{O}(n)}{2\pi}.

(75)

Since all weights are
\pm 1
and each
\gamma
term is prefactored by 2, the period of the optimisation function is
T_{\gamma}=\pi
. Utilising the maximum frequency and the period of the function, sampling period is determined from
theorem

4
.
∎

D.2
Triangle-Free Graphs

Proof of
Corollary

4
.

For a triangle-free graph, the maximum angular frequency of the
\gamma
terms in the expectation value is given by:

\omega_{\max}\left[\left\langle C_{uv}\right\rangle_{\gamma}\right]=2|J_{uv}|+2\times\max\left\{\sum_{w\in e}|J_{wv}|,\sum_{w\in d}|J_{uw}|\right\}.

(76)

Since the graph is
D
-regular, each vertex has
D-1
neighbours excluding
u
and
v
, so
|e|=|d|=D-1
. Consequently,

\max\left\{\sum_{w\in e}|J_{wv}|,\sum_{w\in d}|J_{uw}|\right\}=D-1,

(77)

which gives the maximum angular frequency as

\omega_{\max}\left[\left\langle C_{uv}\right\rangle_{\gamma}\right]=2+2(D-1)=2D.

(78)

The corresponding maximum frequency is
\omega_{\max}/2\pi=D/\pi
. Since each
\gamma
term is prefactored by 2 and the weights are
\pm 1
, the optimisation function has a period of
T_{\gamma}=\pi
. Using this period and the maximum frequency, the sampling period is derived from
theorem

4
.

For triangle-free graphs with varying degrees, the maximum angular frequency is determined by the edge
{u,v}
where at least one vertex has the maximum degree
D_{\max}
.
∎

Appendix E
Univariate Representation of Level-1 QAOA’s Objective Function

In this section, we present the proofs of the linearised closed-form expressions for computing the expectation value of QAOA
1
, specifically for Ising models with and without external fields, as described in
theorem

5
and
theorem

2
. These proofs rely on identities involving linear combinations of trigonometric functions and simple algebraic techniques.

E.1
Ising Models without Fields

Proof of
Theorem

5
.

Using the definitions of the coefficients
A(\gamma)
and
B(\gamma)
, the expectation value for an arbitrary Ising model without external fields can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\langle\gamma,\beta|H_{P}|\gamma,\beta\rangle

\displaystyle=A(\gamma)\sin 4\beta-B(\gamma)\sin^{2}2\beta

(79)

\displaystyle=A(\gamma)\sin 4\beta-B(\gamma)\left(\frac{1-\cos 4\beta}{2}\right)

(80)

\displaystyle=A(\gamma)\sin 4\beta+\frac{B(\gamma)}{2}\cos 4\beta-\frac{B(\gamma)}{2}.

(81)

Using the trigonometric identity for a linear combination of sine and cosine, specifically:

R\cos(x-\alpha)=A\sin x+B\cos x,

(82)

the expression simplifies to:

\langle\gamma,\beta|H_{P}|\gamma,\beta\rangle=R(\gamma)\cos(4 \beta-\alpha(\gamma))-\frac{B(\gamma)}{2},

(83)

where

R(\gamma)=\mathrm{sgn}\left[B(\gamma)\right]\sqrt{A^{2}(\gamma)+\frac{B^{2}(\gamma)}{4}},\quad\alpha(\gamma)=\arctan\left(2A(\gamma),B(\gamma)\right).

(84)

The minimum value of
\langle\gamma,\beta|H_{P}|\gamma,\beta\rangle
is thus:

\underset{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}}{\min}\left(-\sqrt{A^{2}(\gamma)+\frac{B^{2}(\gamma)}{4}}-\frac{B(\gamma)}{2}\right),

(85)

which is achieved by setting
\beta^{*}=\dfrac{\alpha(\gamma^{*})+\pi}{4}
.
∎

E.2
Ising Models with Fields

Proof of
Theorem

6
.

Using the definitions of the coefficients
A(\gamma),B(\gamma)
, and
C(\gamma)
, the expectation value for an arbitrary 2-local Ising model with external fields can be rewritten as:

\langle\gamma,\beta|H_{P}|\gamma,\beta\rangle=\sin(2 \beta)A(\gamma)+\sin(4 \beta)B(\gamma)+\sin^{2}(2\beta)C(\gamma).

(86)

To minimise this expression with respect to
\beta
for a fixed
\gamma
, consider:

\left\langle\beta|H_{\gamma}|\beta\right\rangle=A\sin 2\beta+B\sin 4\beta+C\sin^{2}2\beta.

(87)

This function is bounded with endpoints
\left\langle 0\left|H_{\gamma}\right|0\right\rangle=\left\langle\pi\left|H_{\gamma}\right|\pi\right\rangle=0
and has a period of
\pi
. To find the global minimum in
(0,\pi)
, let
b=2\beta
:

\left\langle\beta|H_{\gamma}|\beta\right\rangle=A\sin b+B\sin 2b+C\sin^{2}b.

(88)

Taking the derivative with respect to
\beta
:

\displaystyle\frac{d\left\langle\beta|H_{\gamma}|\beta\right\rangle}{d\beta}

\displaystyle=2\left[A\cos b+2B\cos 2b+2C\sin b\cos b\right]

(89)

\displaystyle=2\left[A\cos b+2B(2\cos^{2}b-1)+2C\sin b\cos b\right]

(90)

\displaystyle=2\left[A\cos b+4B\cos^{2}b-2B+2C\sin b\cos b\right].

(91)

Setting
\dfrac{d\left\langle\beta|H_{\gamma}|\beta\right\rangle}{d\beta}=0
to find the minima:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=A\cos b+4B\cos^{2}b-2B+2C\sin b\cos b

(92)

\displaystyle 4C^{2}\sin^{2}b\cos^{2}b

\displaystyle=(A\cos b+4B\cos^{2}b-2B)^{2}

(93)

\displaystyle 4C^{2}(1-\cos^{2}b)\cos^{2}b

\displaystyle=(A\cos b+4B\cos^{2}b-2B)^{2}.

(94)

Letting
x=\cos b
, we get:

4C^{2}\left(1-x^{2}\right)x^{2}=\left(Ax+4Bx^{2}-2B\right)^{2},

(95)

which simplifies to the quartic equation:

0=p(x)=4B^{2}-4ABx+(A^{2}-16B^{2}-4C^{2})x^{2}+8ABx^{3}+(16B^{2}+4C^{2})x^{4}.

(96)

Let
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}
be the roots of
p(x)
. The minimum of
\left\langle\beta\left|H_{\gamma}\right|\beta\right\rangle
occurs at one of the
\beta
values where
x=\cos(2 \beta)
is a root of
p(x)
. Therefore:

\beta_{\gamma}^{*}=\underset{\beta\in\mathcal{B}_{\gamma}}{\text{argmin}}\left\langle\beta|H_{\gamma}|\beta\right\rangle,

(97)

where

\mathcal{B}_{\gamma}=\left\{\left.\beta=\pm\frac{1}{2}\arccos\left(x_{i}\right)\right\rvert\,i=1,2,3,4\right\}.

(98)

Finally, the global optimal angles
\left(\gamma^{*},\beta^{*}\right)
are given by:

\left(\gamma^{*},\beta^{*}\right)=\underset{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}}{\text{argmin}}\left\langle\gamma,\beta_{\gamma}^{*}|H_{P}|\gamma,\beta_{\gamma}^{*}\right\rangle.

(99)

∎

Appendix F
Proof of
Proposition

1

We present the proof of
proposition

1
, demonstrating that transforming an Ising model with fields into one without them can actually increase the complexity of finding optimal parameters, particularly in bounded-degree graphs where the maximum degree is much smaller than the number of vertices.

Proof of
proposition

1
.

From
theorem

2
, the maximum angular frequency for an Ising model with external fields is given by:

\omega_{\max}\left[\langle H_{P}\rangle_{\gamma}\right]\!=\!\max\left\{\omega_{\max}\left[\langle C_{i}\rangle_{\gamma}\right],\;\omega_{\max}\left[\langle C_{uv}\rangle_{\gamma}\right]\!\Big|\forall i\in V,\;\forall\{u,v\}\in E\right\}.

(100)

For a triangle-free graph with bounded maximum degree
D_{\max}
, it follows that:

\omega_{\max}\left[\langle C_{i}\rangle_{\gamma}\right]=\omega_{\max}\left[\langle C_{uv}\rangle_{\gamma}\right]=2(D_{\max}+1).

(101)

Applying
theorem

4
, we derive the upper bound on the permissible spacing between consecutive samples:

\Delta_{\gamma}<\frac{\pi}{2(D_{\max}+1)}.

(102)

When converting an Ising model with external fields of size
n
(i.e.,
n
spins or vertices) to an equivalent Ising model without external fields, the maximum degree of the resulting graph increases from
D_{\max}
to
n
. According to
corollary

2
, the maximum angular frequency for an Ising model without external fields can be computed using
eq.

35
. Although the resulting graph may contain triangles, the maximum contribution to the angular frequency arises from the non-triangle terms. Therefore, the maximum angular frequency simplifies to:

\displaystyle\omega_{\max}\left[\langle C_{uv}\rangle_{\gamma}\right]

\displaystyle=2\left(|J_{uv}|+\max\left\{\sum_{w\in e}|J_{wv}|,\sum_{w\in d}|J_{uw}|\right\}\right)

(103)

\displaystyle=2\left(1+\sum_{w\in d}|J_{uw}|\right)

(104)

\displaystyle=2\left(1+(n-1)\right)

(105)

\displaystyle=2n.

(106)

Applying
theorem

4
again, we obtain the bound on the maximum permissible spacing between consecutive samples for the transformed model:

\Delta_{\gamma}^{\prime}<\frac{\pi}{2n}.

(107)

Taking the ratio of
\Delta_{\gamma}
to
\Delta_{\gamma}^{\prime}
, we have:

\frac{\Delta_{\gamma}}{\Delta_{\gamma}^{\prime}}<\frac{n}{D_{\max}+1}.

(108)

Thus, when an Ising model with external fields, whose underlying graph is triangle-free with maximum degree
D_{\max}
, is converted to an equivalent model without external fields, the maximum permissible spacing between consecutive samples decreases by a factor of
\frac{n}{D_{\max}+1}
.
∎

Appendix G
Steckin’s Lemma for the Subdivision Algorithm

In this section, we introduce Steckin’s Lemma and demonstrate its application as a rejection criterion in the subdivision algorithm for estimating the maximum modulus of trigonometric polynomials and identifying their optima. We begin by presenting Steckin’s Lemma for univariate real-valued trigonometric polynomials:

Lemma 10

(Steckin’s Lemma)
.

Suppose that the polynomial

f(t)=\sum_{|n|\leq N}\alpha_{n}e^{int}\quad\left(\alpha_{n}\in\mathbf{C}\right)

(109)

is real-valued and that
t_{0}\in[0,2\pi]
satisfies
f(t_{0})=\|f\|_{\infty}
. Then

f\left(t_{0}+s\right)\geq\|f\|_{\infty}\cos Ns\quad(|s|\leq\pi/N).

(110)

The proof of
lemma

10
is outlined in Exercise 1.8 of
[
111
]
. Stecklin’s Lemma offers valuable insight into the behaviour of real-valued trigonometric polynomials at their maximum points. Specifically, it states that if a polynomial
f(t)
attains its maximum value at
t_{0}
, then within a neighbourhood around
t_{0}
(where the distance
s
satisfies
|s|\leq\pi/N
), the polynomial does not decrease below
\|f\|_{\infty}\cos(N s)
. This ensures that
f(t)
retains a significant portion of its maximum value near
t_{0}
. Such a property is crucial in applications like signal processing and approximation theory, where understanding the local behaviour of trigonometric polynomials is essential for accurate analysis and optimisation.

Now, given an analytic polynomial
p
, we can define its maximum modulus on the unit disk as

\|p\|_{\infty}:=\sup\{|p(z)|:z\in\mathbb{C},|z|\leq 1\}

(111)

Since
\|p\|_{\infty}
is attained by
|p|
on the boundary of the unit disc, our objective is to find bounds on

\sup\left\{\left|p\left(e^{it}\right)\right|:t\in[0,2\pi]\right\},

(112)

and since our calculations are simplified if we work with the square of
|p|
, we write

q(t):=\left|p\left(e^{it}\right)\right|^{2}=p\left(e^{it}\right)\overline{p\left(e^{it}\right)}.

(113)

Suppose
q
has been evaluated at
t_{k}
. If
h<\pi/N
and the global maximiser
t_{0}
lies within the interval
\left[t_{k}-h,t_{k}+h\right]
, then

q\left(t_{k}\right)\geq\|q\|_{\infty}\cos N\left(t_{k}-t_{0}\right)\geq\|q\|_{\infty}\cos Nh

(114)

as shown by
lemma

10
. Consequently, if the global maximiser is known to lie within one of the intervals
I_{1},\ldots,I_{n}
(each of size
h
), and
q
has been evaluated at their midpoints
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
, then there exists some
k
such that
\|q\|_{\infty}\leq q\left(t_{k}\right)\sec Nh
. Define
\tilde{q}:=\max\left\{q\left(t_{i}\right):i=1,\ldots,n\right\}
. Then, the following bounds hold:

\tilde{q}\leq\|q\|_{\infty}\leq\tilde{q}\sec Nh.

(115)

Moreover, if
q\left(t_{i}\right)<\tilde{q}\cos Nh
, the global maximiser cannot lie within the interval
I_{i}
, allowing us to discard it. These observations lead to a straightforward algorithm for calculating
\|p\|_{\infty}
and determining the location of the global optimum, as outlined in algorithm
1
.

Chevrotiere
[
77
]
extended Green’s
[
76
]
work by generalising
lemma

10
to multivariate polynomials and expanding the subdivision algorithm to estimate the maximum modulus of multivariate trigonometric polynomials on polydisks. The Multivariate Stecklin’s Lemma is formalised as follows:

Lemma 11

(Multivariate Steckin’s Lemma)
.

Let
g(t)
be a real-valued multivariate trigonometric polynomial defined by

g(t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left|n_{j}\right|\leq d_{j}\\
j=1,\ldots,k\end{subarray}}c_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}e^{i\left(n_{1}t_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}t_{k}\right)},

(116)

where
t=\left(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}\right)\in[0,2\pi]^{k}
and
c_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}\in\mathbb{C}
. Suppose there exists a point
t_{0}\in[0,2\pi]^{k}
such that
g\left(t_{0}\right)=\|g\|_{\infty}
. Then, for any
s=\left(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
satisfying
d_{1}\left|s_{1}\right|+\cdots+d_{k}\left|s_{k}\right|\leq\pi
, the polynomial
g
satisfies

g\left(t_{0}+s\right)\geq\|g\|_{\infty}\cos\left(d_{1}\left|s_{1}\right|+\cdots+d_{k}\left|s_{k}\right|\right).

(117)

The proof of
lemma

11
is provided in Chevrotiere
[
77
]
. Chevrotiere demonstrates that by using
lemma

11
as a rejection criterion, Green’s
[
76
]
subdivision algorithm can be effectively extended to arbitrary dimensions.

Let us now address the optimisation of the univariate functions defined in
theorem

5
and
theorem

6
. Suppose we optimise over both parameters
(\gamma,\beta)
using the expressions from
theorem

1
and
corollary

1
. In this scenario,
lemma

11
provides the following bound:

\left\langle C(\gamma^{*}+s_{1},\beta^{*}+s_{2})\right\rangle^{2}\geq\left\langle C(\gamma^{*},\beta^{*})\right\rangle^{2}\cos\left(\nu^{\gamma}_{max}|s_{1}|+\nu^{\beta}_{max}|s_{2}|\right),

(118)

where
\nu_{\max}^{\gamma}
and
\nu_{\max}^{\beta}
are the maximum frequencies of the
\gamma
and
\beta
terms, respectively, and
\left|s_{1}\right|
and
\left|s_{2}\right|
denote the distances from the global maximum satisfying
\nu_{\max}^{\gamma}\left|s_{1}\right|+\nu_{\max}^{\beta}\left|s_{2}\right|<\pi
.

However, if we restrict our search to
\bigl(\gamma,\beta^{*}\bigr)
as in
theorem

5
, then
\lvert s_{2}\rvert=0
, and the bound simplifies to the same form as in
eq.

114
. In the context of
theorem

6
, we only know a locally optimal
\beta_{\gamma}^{*}
for each
\gamma
; if
\beta_{\gamma}^{*}=\beta^{*}
for any
\gamma\in[0,\pi]
, then
\lvert s_{2}\rvert=0
again, and the bound remains unchanged. If instead
\lvert s_{2}\rvert\neq 0
(i.e.,
\beta_{\gamma}^{*}\neq\beta^{*}
), then using the fact that
\nu_{\max}^{\gamma}\lvert s_{1}\rvert+\nu_{\max}^{\beta}\lvert s_{2}\rvert<\pi
, it follows that

\cos\bigl(\nu_{\max}^{\gamma}\lvert s_{1}\rvert+\nu_{\max}^{\beta}\lvert s_{2}\rvert\bigr)<\cos\bigl(\nu_{\max}^{\gamma}\lvert s_{1}\rvert\bigr),

(119)

which leads to the rejection of that particular interval. Therefore, in optimising the univariate function of
\gamma
, it suffices to treat both expressions in
theorem

5
and
theorem

6
as univariate trigonometric polynomials. Consequently, we can apply Green’s
[
76
]
subdivision algorithm to efficiently estimate the optimal value
\gamma^{*}
.

Theorem 12
.

Let
p:[0,2\pi]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a real trigonometric polynomial of degree
N
such that
\|p\|_{\infty}=1
. The subdivision algorithm requires
\mathcal{O}(N/\sqrt{\varepsilon})
function evaluations to approximate
\operatorname{max}\,\,|p(x)|^{2}
to within an additive tolerance
\varepsilon>0
.

Proof.

The algorithm begins by subdividing the interval
[0,2\pi]
into
M
subintervals, each of length
\Delta_{\gamma}=\pi/M
, where
M>2N
. Consequently, the initial number of subintervals satisfies
M=\mathcal{O}(N)
. Although the algorithm prunes many subintervals in each iteration—specifically those whose midpoint values are below a certain threshold—it is possible, in the worst case, to retain up to
\mathcal{O}(N)
subintervals.

With each subdivision step, the number of subintervals doubles. Therefore, after
i
iterations, the number of subintervals can grow to
\mathcal{O}\left(N\cdot 2^{i}\right)
. Since the algorithm only evaluates the midpoint of each subinterval during subdivision, the total number of function evaluations after
i
iterations remains bounded by
\mathcal{O}\left(N\cdot 2^{i}\right)
.

The stopping criterion for Green’s algorithm is that the quantity
\tilde{q}\left(\sec(N \Delta_{\gamma})-1\right)
becomes sufficiently small. Here,
\tilde{q}
represents the current estimate of the polynomial’s maximum modulus, and
\Delta_{\gamma}
is the current width of the subintervals. Given that
\|p\|_{\infty}=1
, we approximate
\tilde{q}\approx 1
. For small
\Delta{\gamma}
, we can approximate:

\sec\left(N\Delta_{\gamma}\right)-1\approx\frac{\left(N\Delta_{\gamma}\right)^{2}}{2}.

(120)

To ensure that the algorithm’s error is below a tolerance
\varepsilon
, we require:

\tilde{q}\left(\sec\left(N\Delta_{\gamma}\right)-1\right)\lesssim\varepsilon\quad\Rightarrow\quad\left(N\Delta_{\gamma}\right)^{2}\lesssim\varepsilon\quad\Rightarrow\Delta_{\gamma}\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{N}.

(121)

Since each iteration halves
\Delta_{\gamma}
, starting from
\Delta_{\gamma}^{(0)}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{N}\right)
, after
i
halvings we have:

\Delta_{\gamma}\approx\frac{1}{N}\frac{1}{2^{i}}.

(122)

To satisfy the error tolerance, we set:

\frac{1}{N\cdot 2^{i}}\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{N}\quad\Rightarrow\quad 2^{-i}\lesssim\sqrt{\varepsilon}\quad\Rightarrow\quad 2^{i}\approx\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}.

(123)

Substituting
2^{i}\approx\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}
into the total number of function evaluations, we obtain
\mathcal{O}(N/\sqrt{\varepsilon})
.
∎

Appendix H
Globally Optimal Parameters for Regular Graphs

H.1
Mathematical Preliminaries

H.1.1
Probability Density Functions

In this subsection, we introduce the necessary definitions and notations related to probability density functions (PDFs) that are essential for our proof. We begin by defining the expectation operator for functions of random variables and extend these definitions to multiple variables and arbitrary index sets.

Single-Variable Expectation

Let
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a probability density function (PDF). For a measurable function
g:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, the expectation of
g(x)
with respect to
f
is denoted and defined as

\underset{x\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}[g(x)]=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)g(x)dx.

(124)

This integral represents the average value of
g(x)
when
x
is distributed according to the PDF
f
.

Multi-Variable Expectation for
n
Independent Variables

Consider
n
independent random variables
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}
, each distributed according to the same PDF
f
. We denote the joint expectation over these
n
variables as
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim f^{n}}
. Formally, for a function
g
:
\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, the expectation is defined by

\displaystyle\underset{x\sim f^{n}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[g(x)\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{x_{1}\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\cdots\underset{x_{n}\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[g(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\right]

(125)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f\left(x_{1}\right)f\left(x_{2}\right)\cdots f\left(x_{n}\right)g\left(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\right)dx_{1}dx_{2}\cdots dx_{n}.

(126)

This expression computes the expectation of
g
over the product measure
f^{n}
, reflecting the independence of each
x_{i}
.

Expectation over an Arbitrary Index Set

Let
E
be an arbitrary index set, and for each
e\in E
, let
x_{e}
be a random variable distributed according to the PDF
f
. We define the expectation operator over the collection of variables indexed by
E
as

\underset{x\sim f^{E}}{\mathbb{E}}=\prod_{e\in E}\underset{x_{e}\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}.

(127)

For a function
g:\mathbb{R}^{E}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, the expectation is given by

\displaystyle\underset{x\sim f^{E}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[g(x)\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{e\in E}\underset{x_{e}\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[g\left((e)_{e\in E}\right)\right]

(128)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim f^{E}}[g(x)]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{E}}\prod_{e\in E}f\left(x_{e}\right)g\left(\left(x_{e}\right)_{e\in E}\right)\prod_{e\in E}dx_{e}.

(129)

This generalises the expectation to any index set
E
, allowing for flexibility in handling functions of numerous variables.

H.1.2
Interchanging Summation and Integration in Expectations

Throughout this section, we assume that the probability density functions
p
are chosen such that the infinite sum arising from the Taylor series expansion and the integrals involved in taking expectations can be interchanged. This assumption is crucial for the validity of the subsequent manipulations.

Consider a function
f
expressed as its Taylor series expansion:

f(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}x^{i}

(130)

Under our assumption, we can compute the expectation of
f(x)
with respect to the probability distribution
p
by interchanging the summation and the integral. The steps are as follows:

\displaystyle\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[f(x)\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}x^{i}\right]

(131)

\displaystyle=\int dx\,p(x)\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}x^{i}

(132)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\int dx\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}}_{=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\int dx}p(x)a_{i}x^{i}

(133)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}\int dx\,p(x)x^{i}

(134)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{i}\right].

(135)

H.2
Some Useful Lemmas

Lemma 13
.

Let
x
be a random variable distributed according to
p(x)
, and let
D
be a large parameter. Then the expectation of
x\sin\left(\frac{ax}{\sqrt{D}}\right)
can be approximated by:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x\sim p}\left[x\sin\left(\frac{ax}{\sqrt{D}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{\sqrt{D}}\mathbb{E}\left[h^{2}\right]-\frac{a^{3}}{6D^{3/2}}\mathbb{E}\left[h^{4}\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-\frac{5}{2}}\right),

(136)

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{\sqrt{D}}\mathbb{E}\left[h^{2}\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-\frac{3}{2}}\right).

Proof.

\displaystyle\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x\sin\frac{ax}{\sqrt{D}}\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
\text{ odd }\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{\frac{k-1}{2}}}{k!}\left(\frac{ax}{\sqrt{D}}\right)^{k}\right]\qquad\left(\text{using }\sin x=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
\text{ odd }\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{\frac{k-1}{2}}}{k!}x^{k}\right)

(137)

\displaystyle=\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
\text{odd}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{\frac{k-1}{2}}}{k!}\frac{a^{k}}{D^{\frac{k}{2}}}\,x^{k+1}\right]

(138)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
\text{odd}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{(-1)^{\frac{k-1}{2}}}{k!}\frac{a^{k}}{D^{\frac{k}{2}}}\,x^{k+1}\right]

(139)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
\text{odd}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{\frac{k-1}{2}}}{k!}\frac{a^{k}}{D^{\frac{k}{2}}}\,\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{k+1}\right]

(140)

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{\sqrt{D}}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[h^{2}\right]-\frac{1}{3!}\frac{a^{3}}{D^{\frac{3}{2}}}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[h^{4}\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{\frac{5}{2}}}\right)

(141)

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{\sqrt{D}}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[h^{2}\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{\frac{3}{2}}}\right).

(142)

∎

Lemma 14
.

Let
a
and
b
be parameters independent of
D
, and let
D
be a large parameter. Then the following approximations hold:

\left[1+\frac{a}{D}+\frac{b}{D^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right)\right]^{D}=e^{a}\left(1+\left(b-\frac{a^{2}}{2}\right)\frac{1}{D}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right)\right),

(143)

and:

\left[1+\frac{a}{D}+\frac{b}{D^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right)\right]^{D+1}=e^{a}\left(1+\frac{a+b-\frac{a^{2}}{2}}{D}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right)\right).

(144)

Proof.

Using the Taylor expansion:

\ln(1 + x)=x-\frac{x^{2}}{2}+\mathcal{O}(x^{3}),

(145)

we expand:

\displaystyle\ln\left(1+\frac{a}{D}+\frac{b}{D^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right)\!\right)\!

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{D}+\frac{b}{D^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right)\!-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{a}{D}+\frac{b}{D^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right)\!\right)^{2}\hskip-7.11317pt+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right)

(146)

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{D}+\frac{b}{D^{2}}-\frac{a^{2}}{2D^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right)

(147)

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{D}+\left(b-\frac{a^{2}}{2}\right)\frac{1}{D^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right).

(148)

Multiplying by
D
, we find:

D\ln\left(1+\frac{a}{D}+\frac{b}{D^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right)\right)=a+\left(b-\frac{a^{2}}{2}\right)\frac{1}{D}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right).

(149)

1.

For the first result, we compute:

\displaystyle\left[1+\frac{a}{D}+\frac{b}{D^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right)\right]^{D}

\displaystyle=e^{D\ln\left(1+\frac{a}{D}+\frac{b}{D^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right)\right)}

(150)

\displaystyle=e^{a+\left(b-\frac{a^{2}}{2}\right)\frac{1}{D}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right)}

(151)

\displaystyle=e^{a}\cdot e^{\left(b-\frac{a^{2}}{2}\right)\frac{1}{D}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right)}

(152)

\displaystyle=e^{a}\left(1+\left(b-\frac{a^{2}}{2}\right)\frac{1}{D}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right)\right).

(153)

where the Taylor expansion:

e^{x}=1+x+\frac{x^{2}}{2}+\mathcal{O}(x^{3})

is applied in the last step.

2.

For the second result:

\displaystyle\left[1+\frac{a}{D}+\frac{b}{D^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right)\right]^{D+1}

\displaystyle=e^{a}\left(1+\left(b-\frac{a^{2}}{2}\right)\frac{1}{D}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right)\right)\left[1+\frac{a}{D}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right)\right]

(154)

\displaystyle=e^{a}\left(1+\left(b-\frac{a^{2}}{2}\right)\frac{1}{D}+\frac{a}{D}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right)\right)

(155)

\displaystyle=e^{a}\left(1+\frac{a+b-\frac{a^{2}}{2}}{D}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right)\right).

(156)

∎

Lemma 15
.

Let
x
be a random variable distributed according to
p(x)
, and let
D
be a large parameter. Then the following approximations hold:

1.

The expectation of
\cos\left(\frac{ax}{\sqrt{D}}\right)
is given by:

\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\frac{ax}{\sqrt{D}}\right]=1-\frac{1}{2}\frac{a^{2}}{D}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]+\frac{1}{4!}\frac{a^{4}}{D^{2}}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{4}\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right).

(157)

2.

For the
D
-th power of expectation:

\displaystyle\left(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\frac{ax}{\sqrt{D}}\right]\right)^{D}

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{1}{2}a^{2}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]}\!\left[1+\frac{1}{8}a^{4}\left(\frac{1}{3}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{4}\right]\!-\!\left(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]\right)^{2}\right)\frac{1}{D}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right)\right]

(158)

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{1}{2}a^{2}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]}\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D}\right)\right).

3.

For the
(D+1)
-th power of expectation:

\displaystyle\left(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\frac{ax}{\sqrt{D}}\right]\right)^{D+1}

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{1}{2}a^{2}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]}\Bigg[1+\!\Bigg(\!-\frac{1}{2}a^{2}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]\!+\frac{1}{4!}a^{4}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{4}\right]

(159)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{8}a^{4}\left(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]\right)^{2}\Bigg)\frac{1}{D}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right)\Bigg]

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{1}{2}a^{2}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]}\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D}\right)\right).

Proof.

1.

For the first result, we compute:

\displaystyle\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\frac{ax}{\sqrt{D}}\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=0\\
k\text{ even }\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k/2}}{k!}\left(\frac{ax}{\sqrt{D}}\right)^{k}\right]\quad\left(\text{using }\cos x=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=0\\
k\text{ even }\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k/2}}{k!}x^{k}\right)

(160)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=0\\
k\text{ even }\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{(-1)^{k/2}}{k!}\left(\frac{ax}{\sqrt{D}}\right)^{k}\right]

(161)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=0\\
k\text{ even }\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k/2}}{k!}\frac{a^{k}}{D^{k/2}}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{k}\right]

(162)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{2}\frac{a^{2}}{D}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]+\frac{1}{4!}\frac{a^{4}}{D^{2}}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{4}\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right).

(163)

2.

Using the
eq.

157
, we expand its
D
-th power:

\left(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\frac{ax}{\sqrt{D}}\right]\right)^{D}=\left[1-\frac{1}{2}\frac{a^{2}}{D}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]+\frac{1}{4!}\frac{a^{4}}{D^{2}}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{4}\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right)\right]^{D}

(164)

Let:

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}a^{2}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right],

(165)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4!}a^{4}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{4}\right].

(166)

Then:

\displaystyle t-\frac{s^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4!}a^{4}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{4}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\left(-\frac{1}{2}a^{2}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]\right)^{2}

(167)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4!}a^{4}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{4}\right]-\frac{1}{8}a^{4}\left(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]\right)^{2}

(168)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}a^{4}\left[\frac{1}{3}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{4}\right]-\left(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]\right)^{2}\right].

(169)

Using
eq.

143
of lemma
14
, we find:

\left(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\frac{ax}{\sqrt{D}}\right]\right)^{D}=e^{-\frac{1}{2}a^{2}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]}\!\left[1+\frac{1}{8}a^{4}\!\left(\frac{1}{3}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{4}\right]\!-\!\left(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]\right)^{2}\right)\frac{1}{D}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right)\right].

(170)

3.

Similarly, for the
(D+1)
-th power:

\left(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\frac{ax}{\sqrt{D}}\right]\right)^{D+1}=\left[1-\frac{1}{2}\frac{a^{2}}{D}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]+\frac{1}{4!}\frac{a^{4}}{D^{2}}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{4}\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right)\right]^{D+1}.

(171)

Using the definitions of
s
and
t
from
eq.

165
and
eq.

166
, we compute:

s+t-\frac{s^{2}}{2}=-\frac{1}{2}a^{2}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]+\frac{1}{8}a^{4}\left[\frac{1}{3}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{4}\right]-\left(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]\right)^{2}\right].

(172)

Using the
eq.

144
of lemma
14
, we get:

\displaystyle\left(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\frac{ax}{\sqrt{D}}\right]\right)^{D+1}

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{1}{2}a^{2}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]}\Bigg[\!1\!+\!\Bigg(\!-\frac{1}{2}a^{2}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\!\left[x^{2}\right]\!+\!\frac{1}{4!}a^{4}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\!\left[x^{4}\right]

(173)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{8}a^{4}\left(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]\right)^{2}\Bigg)\frac{1}{D}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right)\Bigg].

∎

Lemma 16
.

Let
x
and
x^{\prime}
be independent random variables distributed according to
p(x)
, and let
D
be a large parameter. Then the following approximation holds:

\underset{x,x^{\prime}\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(\left(x\pm x^{\prime}\right)\frac{a}{\sqrt{D}}\right)\right]=1-\frac{a^{2}}{D}\left[\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]\pm(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x\right])^{2}\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right).

(174)

Proof.

Using
eq.

157
of lemma
15
, we have:

\underset{x,x^{\prime}\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(\left(x\pm x^{\prime}\right)\frac{a}{\sqrt{D}}\right)\right]=1-\frac{1}{2}\frac{a^{2}}{D}\underbrace{\underset{x,x^{\prime}\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(x\pm x^{\prime}\right)^{2}\right]}_{\hbox to12.05pt{\vbox to12.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.02568pt\lower-6.02568pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.06 0 C 8.06 4.45 4.45 8.06 0 8.06 C -4.45 8.06 -8.06 4.45 -8.06 0 C -8.06 -4.45 -4.45 -8.06 0 -8.06 C 4.45 -8.06 8.06 -4.45 8.06 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.99306pt}{-2.25555pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.76 -3.12)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right).

(175)

Expanding
1
, we get:

\displaystyle\underset{x,x^{\prime}\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(x\pm x^{\prime}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{x,x^{\prime}\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}+x^{\prime 2}\pm 2xx^{\prime}\right]

(176)

\displaystyle=\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]+\underset{x^{\prime}\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{\prime 2}\right]\pm 2\left(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x\right]\right)\left(\underset{x^{\prime}\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{\prime}\right]\right)

(177)

\displaystyle=2\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]\pm 2\left(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x\right]\right)^{2}.

(178)

Substituting
1
back, we obtain:

\displaystyle\underset{x,x^{\prime}\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(\left(x\pm x^{\prime}\right)\frac{a}{\sqrt{D}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{2}\frac{a^{2}}{D}\left[2\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]\pm 2\left(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x\right]\right)^{2}\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right)

(179)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{a^{2}}{D}\left[\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]\pm\left(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x\right]\right)^{2}\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right).

(180)

∎

Lemma 17
.

Let
a,b,c\in\mathbb{R}
, and let
\lambda\in[0,1]
. For a large parameter
D
, the following approximation holds:

\left[1+\frac{a}{D}+\frac{b}{D^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right)\right]^{cD^{\lambda}}=\begin{cases}e^{ac}\left(1+c\left(b-\frac{a^{2}}{2}\right)D^{-1}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-2}\right)\right)&\text{if }\lambda=1,\\[14.22636pt]
1+acD^{\lambda-1}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{2\lambda-2}\right)&\text{if }0\leq\lambda<1.\end{cases}

(181)

Proof.

Recall from the proof of lemma
14
, we know that:

\ln\left(1+\frac{a}{D}+\frac{b}{D^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right)\right)=\frac{a}{D}+\left(b-\frac{a^{2}}{2}\right)\frac{1}{D^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right).

(182)

Multiplying by
cD^{\lambda}
, we obtain:

cD^{\lambda}\ln\left(1+\frac{a}{D}+\frac{b}{D^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right)\right)=c\left[aD^{\lambda-1}+\left(b-\frac{a^{2}}{2}\right)D^{\lambda-2}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{\lambda-3}\right)\right].

(183)

Expanding the LHS of
eq.

181
, we get:

\displaystyle\left[1+\frac{a}{D}+\frac{b}{D^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right)\right]^{cD^{\lambda}}

\displaystyle=\left[1+\frac{a}{D}+\frac{b}{D^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right)\right]^{cD^{\lambda}}

(184)

\displaystyle=e^{cD^{\lambda}\ln\left(1+\frac{a}{D}+\frac{b}{D^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right)\right)}

(185)

\displaystyle=e^{c\left[aD^{\lambda-1}+\left(b-\frac{a^{2}}{2}\right)D^{\lambda-2}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{\lambda-3}\right)\right]}.

(186)

We now handle two cases based on the value of
\lambda
.

Case 1:
\lambda=1

\displaystyle\left[1+\frac{a}{D}+\frac{b}{D^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right)\right]^{cD^{\lambda}}

\displaystyle=e^{c\left[a+\left(b-\frac{a^{2}}{2}\right)D^{-1}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-2}\right)\right]}

(187)

\displaystyle=e^{ac}e^{c\left(b-\frac{a^{2}}{2}\right)D^{-1}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-2}\right)}

(188)

\displaystyle=e^{ac}\left(1+c\left(b-\frac{a^{2}}{2}\right)D^{-1}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-2}\right)\right).

(189)

Case 2:
\lambda<1

\displaystyle\left[1+\frac{a}{D}+\frac{b}{D^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right)\right]^{cD^{\lambda}}\hskip-14.22636pt

\displaystyle=e^{c\left[aD^{\lambda-1}+\left(b-\frac{a^{2}}{2}\right)D^{\lambda-2}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{\lambda-3}\right)\right]}

(190)

\displaystyle=1+c\left[aD^{\lambda-1}+\left(b-\frac{a^{2}}{2}\right)D^{\lambda-2}+O\left(D^{\lambda-3}\right)\right]

(191)

\displaystyle\quad+c^{2}\left[aD^{\lambda-1}+\left(b-\frac{a^{2}}{2}\right)D^{\lambda-2}+O\left(D^{\lambda-3}\right)\right]^{2}\!+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{3(\lambda-1)}\right)

(192)

\displaystyle=1+acD^{\lambda-1}+a^{2}c^{2}D^{2\lambda-2}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{\lambda-2}\right)

(193)

\displaystyle=1+acD^{\lambda-1}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{2\lambda-2}\right).

(194)

∎

Lemma 18
.

Let
x
be a random variable distributed according to
p(x)
, with
c\in\mathbb{R},a
independent of
D
, and
\lambda\in[0,1]
. For large
D
, the following approximation holds:

\left(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\frac{ax}{\sqrt{D}}\right]\!\right)^{cD^{\lambda}}\hskip-17.07182pt=\begin{cases}e^{-\frac{c}{2}a^{2}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]}\hskip-2.84544pt\left[\!1\!+\!\frac{1}{8}a^{4}c\left(\!\frac{1}{3}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\!\left[x^{4}\right]\!-\!\left(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\!\left[x^{2}\right]\right)^{2}\right)\!\frac{1}{D}\!+\!\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right)\!\right]&\lambda=1,\\[14.22636pt]
1-\frac{c}{2}a^{2}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]D^{\lambda-1}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{2\lambda-2}\right)&0\leqslant\lambda<1.\end{cases}

(195)

Proof.

Raising
eq.

157
of lemma
15
to the power
cD^{\lambda}
, we have:

\left(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\frac{ax}{\sqrt{D}}\right]\right)^{cD^{\lambda}}=\left[1-\frac{1}{2}\frac{a^{2}}{D}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]+\frac{1}{4!}\frac{a^{4}}{D^{2}}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{4}\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3}}\right)\right]^{cD^{\lambda}}.

(196)

Using lemma
17
, we evaluate the expression for the two cases:

Case 1:
\lambda=1

\left(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\frac{ax}{\sqrt{D}}\right]\right)^{cD^{\lambda}}=e^{-\frac{c}{2}a^{2}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]}\left[1+\frac{1}{8}a^{4}c\left(\frac{1}{3}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{4}\right]-\left(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]\right)^{2}\right)\frac{1}{D}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right)\right].

(197)

Case 2:
0\geq\lambda<1

\left(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\frac{ax}{\sqrt{D}}\right]\right)^{cD^{\lambda}}=1-\frac{c}{2}a^{2}\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]D^{\lambda-1}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{2\lambda-2}\right).

(198)

∎

Lemma 19
.

Let
x
and
x^{\prime}
be independent random variables distributed according to
p(x)
, with
c\in\mathbb{R},a
independent of
D
, and
\lambda\in[0,1]
. For large
D
, the following approximation holds:

\left[\underset{x,x^{\prime}\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\!\left[\cos\!\left(\!\left(x\pm x^{\prime}\right)\frac{a}{\sqrt{D}}\right)\!\right]\!\right]^{cD^{\lambda}}\hskip-14.22636pt=\hskip-2.84544pt\begin{cases}e^{-a^{2}c\left[\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]\pm(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x\right])^{2}\right]}\!\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-1}\right)\right)&\text{if }\lambda=1,\\[14.22636pt]
1\!-\!a^{2}c\!\left[\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]\!\pm\!(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x\right])^{2}\right]\!D^{\lambda-1}\!+\mathcal{O}\!\left(D^{2\lambda-2}\right)&\text{if }0\leqslant\lambda<1.\end{cases}

(199)

Proof.

Raising
eq.

174
of lemma
16
to the power
cD^{\lambda}
, we get:

\left[\underset{x,x^{\prime}\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(\left(x\pm x^{\prime}\right)\frac{a}{\sqrt{D}}\right)\right]\right]^{cD^{\lambda}}\hskip-14.22636pt=\left[1-\frac{a^{2}}{D}\left[\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x^{2}\right]\pm(\underset{x\sim p}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x\right])^{2}\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right)\right]^{cD^{\lambda}}.

(200)

Using lemma
17
, we obtain the stated result.
∎

H.3
Proof of
Theorem

7

Before proceeding with the proof of
theorem

7
, we redefine some notations introduced earlier in the manuscript for improved clarity and compactness. These adjustments facilitate a more streamlined presentation of the proof. Specifically, for an edge
\{u,v\}\in E
in a graph
G=(V,E)
, we define:

•

\mathcal{N}_{v\setminus u}=\mathcal{N}(v)\setminus\{u\}
: the set of vertices connected to
v
excluding
u
.

•

\mathcal{N}_{u\setminus v}=\mathcal{N}(u)\setminus\{v\}
: the set of vertices connected to
u
excluding
v
.

•

\mathcal{N}_{v\setminus\!\!\setminus u}=\{w\in\mathcal{N}(v)\mid w\neq u\text{ and }w\notin\mathcal{N}(u)\}
: the neighbours of
v
that are not neighbours of
u
.

•

\mathcal{N}_{u\setminus\!\!\setminus v}=\{w\in\mathcal{N}(u)\mid w\neq v\text{ and }w\notin\mathcal{N}(v)\}
: the neighbours of
u
that are not neighbours of
v
.

•

\mathcal{N}_{uv}=\mathcal{N}_{u\setminus v}\cap\mathcal{N}_{v\setminus u}=\mathcal{N}(u)\cap\mathcal{N}(v)
: the set of vertices that form a triangle with the edge
\{u,v\}
.

Here,
\mathcal{N}(w)=\{x\in V\mid\{x,w\}\in E\}
denotes the set of neighbours of a vertex
w
.

Proof.

Recall that the QAOA
1
cost function for an Ising model defined on a graph
G=(V,E)
with respect to parameters
\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
is

\xi_{G}(\gamma,\beta)=\sum_{i\in V}C_{i}(\gamma,\beta;G)+\sum_{\{u,v\}\in E}C_{uv}^{(1)}(\gamma,\beta;G)+\sum_{\{u,v\}\in E}C_{uv}^{(2)}(\gamma,\beta;G).

(201)

where the functions
C_{i},C_{uv}^{(1)}
, and
C_{uv}^{(2)}
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle C_{i}(\gamma,\beta:G)

\displaystyle=h_{i}\sin(2 \beta)\sin\left(2h_{i}\gamma\right)\prod_{k\in\mathcal{N}(i)}\cos\left(2J_{ik}\gamma\right),

(202)

\displaystyle C_{uv}^{(1)}(\gamma,\beta;G)

\displaystyle=\frac{J_{uv}}{2}\sin(4 \beta)\sin\left(2J_{uv}\gamma\right)\hskip-2.84544pt\left\{\hskip-2.84544pt\cos\left(2h_{v}\gamma\right)\hskip-9.95863pt\prod_{w\in\mathcal{N}_{v\setminus u}}\hskip-9.95863pt\cos\left(2J_{wv}\gamma\right)+\cos\left(2h_{u}\gamma\right)\hskip-9.95863pt\prod_{w\in\mathcal{N}_{u\setminus v}}\hskip-9.95863pt\cos\left(2J_{uw}\gamma\right)\hskip-2.84544pt\right\},

(203)

\displaystyle C_{uv}^{(2)}(\gamma,\beta;G)

\displaystyle=-\frac{J_{uv}}{2}\sin^{2}(2\beta)\hskip-8.5359pt\prod_{w\in N_{v\setminus\!\!\setminus u}}\hskip-8.5359pt\cos\left(2J_{wv}\gamma\right)\hskip-7.11317pt\prod_{w\in N_{u\setminus\!\!\setminus v}}\hskip-7.11317pt\cos\left(2J_{uw}\gamma\right)

(204)

\displaystyle\hskip-59.75095pt\times\left\{\cos\left(2\left(h_{u}+h_{v}\right)\gamma\right)\hskip-5.69046pt\prod_{f\in N_{uv}}\hskip-5.69046pt\cos\left(2\left(J_{uf}+J_{vf}\right)\gamma\right)-\cos\left(2\left(h_{u}-h_{v}\right)\gamma\right)\hskip-5.69046pt\prod_{f\in N_{uv}}\hskip-5.69046pt\cos\left(2\left(J_{uf}-J_{vf}\right)\gamma\right)\right\}.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
g:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be probability density functions. Consider a graph
G=(V,E)
where the edge weights
J_{uv}
are independently drawn from
f
and the vertex weights
h_{i}
are independently drawn from
g
. The expected QAOA
1
cost function for
G
with parameters
\beta
and
\gamma
is then

\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}J\sim f^{E}\\
h\sim g^{V}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\xi_{G}(\gamma,\beta)\right]=\sum_{i\in V}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}J\sim f^{E}\\
h\sim g^{V}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[C_{i}(\gamma,\beta:G)\right]+\hskip-7.11317pt\sum_{\{u,v\}\in E}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}J\sim f^{E}\\
h\sim g^{V}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left\{C_{uv}^{(1)}(\gamma,\beta;G)+\hskip-7.11317pt\sum_{\{u,v\}\in E}C_{uv}^{(2)}(\gamma,\beta;G)\right\}.

(205)

To facilitate the analysis, we decompose the expectation into separate terms:

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}J\sim f^{E}\\
h\sim g^{V}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[C_{i}(\gamma,\beta:G)\right],

(206)

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}J\sim f^{E}\\
h\sim g^{V}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[C_{uv}^{(1)}(\gamma,\beta;G)\right],

(207)

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}J\sim f^{E}\\
h\sim g^{V}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[C_{uv}^{(2)}(\gamma,\beta;G)\right].

(208)

We will compute each of these terms individually to understand their contributions to the overall expected cost function.

Step 1: Computing the Term
T_{1}

We begin by evaluating the expectation
T_{1}
of the term
C_{i}(\gamma,\beta;G)
over the distributions of the edge weights
J\sim f^{E}
and vertex weights
h\sim g^{V}
:

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}J\sim f^{E}\\
h\sim g^{V}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[C_{i}(\gamma,\beta:G)\right]

(209)

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}J\sim f^{E}\\
h\sim g^{V}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{i}\sin(2\beta)\sin(2h_i\gamma)\prod_{k\in\mathcal{N}(i)}\cos(2J_{ik}\gamma)\right]

(210)

\displaystyle=\sin(4\beta)\underset{h\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[h\sin(2h\gamma)\right]\prod_{k\in\mathcal{N}(i)}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos(2J\gamma)\right]

(211)

\displaystyle=\sin(4\beta)\underset{h\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[h\sin(2h\gamma)\right]\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos(2J\gamma)\right]\right)^{|\mathcal{N}(i)|}.

(212)

Since the graph
G
is
(D+1)
-regular, each vertex
i
has
|\mathcal{N}(i)|=D+1
neighbors. By setting
\gamma=\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}
, the expression for
T_{1}
simplifies to:

T_{1}=\sin(4\beta)\underbrace{\underset{h\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[h\sin(2h\gamma)\right]}_{\hbox to12.05pt{\vbox to12.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.02568pt\lower-6.02568pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.99306pt}{-2.25555pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.76 -3.12)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\underbrace{\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos(2J\gamma)\right]\right)^{D+1}}_{\hbox to12.05pt{\vbox to12.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.02568pt\lower-6.02568pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.06 0 C 8.06 4.45 4.45 8.06 0 8.06 C -4.45 8.06 -8.06 4.45 -8.06 0 C -8.06 -4.45 -4.45 -8.06 0 -8.06 C 4.45 -8.06 8.06 -4.45 8.06 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.99306pt}{-2.25555pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.76 -3.12)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}.

(213)

Next, we apply
lemma

13
and
lemma

15
to approximate the terms
1
and
2
as follows:

1

\displaystyle=\underset{h\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[h\sin\frac{2h\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}\right]=\frac{2\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}\underset{h\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[h^{2}\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3/2}}\right),

(214)

2

\displaystyle=\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos(2J\gamma)\right]\right)^{D+1}=e^{-2\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D}\right)\right).

(215)

Substituting
1
and
2
back into the expression for
T_{1}
, we obtain:

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=\sin(4\beta)\left[\frac{2\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}\underset{h\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[h^{2}\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3/2}}\right)\right]e^{-2\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D}\right)\right)

(216)

\displaystyle=\sin(4\beta)\underset{h\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[h^{2}\right]\frac{2\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}e^{-2\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D}\right).

(217)

Step 2: Computing the Term
T_{2}

Next, we evaluate the expectation
T_{2}
of the term
C_{uv}^{(1)}(\gamma,\beta;G)
over the distributions of the edge weights
J\sim f^{E}
and vertex weights
h\sim g^{V}
:

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}J\sim f^{E}\\
h\sim g^{V}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[C_{uv}^{(1)}(\gamma,\beta;G)\right]

(218)

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}J\sim f^{E}\\
h\sim g^{V}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{J_{uv}}{2}\sin(4 \beta)\sin\left(2J_{uv}\gamma\right)\hskip-2.84544pt\left\{\cos\left(2h_{v}\gamma\right)\hskip-9.95863pt\prod_{w\in\mathcal{N}_{v\setminus u}}\hskip-9.95863pt\cos\left(2J_{wv}\gamma\right)+\cos\left(2h_{u}\gamma\right)\hskip-9.95863pt\prod_{w\in\mathcal{N}_{u\setminus v}}\hskip-9.95863pt\cos\left(2J_{uw}\gamma\right)\right\}\right]

(219)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sin(4\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\sin(2J \gamma)\right]\hskip-2.84544pt\Bigg\{\underset{h\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2h\gamma\right)\right]\hskip-11.38092pt\prod_{w\in\mathcal{N}_{v\setminus u}}\hskip-7.11317pt\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2J\gamma\right)\right]\hskip-2.84544pt+\hskip-2.84544pt\underset{h\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2h\gamma\right)\right]\hskip-9.95863pt\prod_{w\in\mathcal{N}_{u\setminus v}}\hskip-7.11317pt\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2J\gamma\right)\right]\hskip-2.84544pt\Bigg\}

(220)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sin(4\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\sin(2J \gamma)\right]\underset{h\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2h\gamma\right)\right]\hskip-2.84544pt\left\{\hskip-4.26773pt\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2J\gamma\right)\right]\hskip-2.84544pt\right)^{|\mathcal{N}_{v\setminus u}|}\hskip-9.95863pt+\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2J\gamma\right)\right]\hskip-2.84544pt\right)^{|\mathcal{N}_{u\setminus v}|}\hskip-1.42271pt\right\}.

(221)

Given that the graph
G
is
(D+1)
-regular, each edge
\{u,v\}
has neighbourhoods satisfying:

|\mathcal{N}_{u\setminus v}|=|\mathcal{N}_{v\setminus u}|=(D+1)-1=D.

(222)

Substituting this into the expression and setting
\gamma=\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}
, we further simplify
T_{2}
as:

T_{2}=\sin(4\beta)\underbrace{\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\sin(2J \frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{D}})\right]}_{\hbox to12.05pt{\vbox to12.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.02568pt\lower-6.02568pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\underbrace{\underset{h\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2h\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}\right)\right]}_{\hbox to12.05pt{\vbox to12.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.02568pt\lower-6.02568pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\underbrace{\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2J\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}\right)\right]\right)^{D}}_{\hbox to12.05pt{\vbox to12.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.02568pt\lower-6.02568pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}.

(223)

Applying
lemma

13
and
lemma

15
, we approximate the terms
1
,
2
, and
3
as follows:

1

\displaystyle=\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\sin(2J \frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{D}})\right]=\frac{2\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{\frac{3}{2}}}\right),

(224)

2

\displaystyle=\underset{h\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2h\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}\right)\right]=1+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D}\right),

(225)

3

\displaystyle=\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2J\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}\right)\right]\right)^{D}=e^{-2\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D}\right)\right).

(226)

Substituting
1
,
2
, and
3
back into the expression for
T_{2}
, we obtain:

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=\sin(4\beta)\left[\frac{2\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{3/2}}\right)\right]e^{-2\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D}\right)\right)

(227)

\displaystyle=\sin(4\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]\frac{2\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}e^{-2\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D}\right).

(228)

Step 3: Computing the Term
T_{3}

We now evaluate the expectation
T_{3}
of the term
C_{uv}^{(2)}(\gamma,\beta;G)
over the distributions of the edge weights
J\sim f^{E}
and vertex weights
h\sim g^{V}
as follows:

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}J\sim f^{E}\\
h\sim g^{V}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[C_{uv}^{(2)}(\gamma,\beta;G)\right]

(229)

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}J\sim f^{E}\\
h\sim g^{V}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left\{-\frac{J_{uv}}{2}\sin^{2}(2\beta)\prod_{w\in N_{v\setminus\!\!\setminus u}}\cos\left(2J_{wv}\gamma\right)\prod_{w\in N_{u\setminus\!\!\setminus v}}\cos\left(2J_{uw}\gamma\right)\right.

(230)

\displaystyle\quad\times\left\{\cos\left(2\left(h_{u}+h_{v}\right)\gamma\right)\hskip-5.69046pt\prod_{f\in N_{uv}}\hskip-5.69046pt\cos\left(2\left(J_{uf}+J_{vf}\right)\gamma\right)\left.-\cos\left(2\left(h_{u}-h_{v}\right)\gamma\right)\hskip-5.69046pt\prod_{f\in N_{uv}}\hskip-5.69046pt\cos\left(2\left(J_{uf}-J_{vf}\right)\gamma\right)\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sin^{2}(2\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\prod_{w\in N_{v\setminus\!\!\setminus u}}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2J\gamma\right)\right]\prod_{w\in N_{u\setminus\!\!\setminus v}}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2J\gamma\right)\right]

(231)

\displaystyle\quad\times\left\{\underset{h,h^{\prime}\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2(h+h^{\prime})\gamma\right)\right]\prod_{f\in N_{uv}}\underset{J,J^{\prime}\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2(J+J^{\prime})\gamma\right)\right]\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.-\underset{h,h^{\prime}\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2(h-h^{\prime})\gamma\right)\right]\prod_{f\in N_{uv}}\underset{J,J^{\prime}\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2(J-J^{\prime})\gamma\right)\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sin^{2}(2\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2J\gamma\right)\right]\right)^{|N_{v\setminus\!\!\setminus u}|+|N_{u\setminus\!\!\setminus v}|}

(232)

\displaystyle\quad\times\left\{\underset{h,h^{\prime}\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2(h+h^{\prime})\gamma\right)\right]\left(\underset{J,J^{\prime}\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2(J+J^{\prime})\gamma\right)\right]\right)^{|\mathcal{N}_{uv}|}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.-\underset{h,h^{\prime}\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2(h-h^{\prime})\gamma\right)\right]\left(\underset{J,J^{\prime}\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2(J-J^{\prime})\gamma\right)\right]\right)^{|\mathcal{N}_{uv}|}\right\}.

Since
G
is
(D+1)
-regular graph, we have:

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}(u)|

\displaystyle=|\mathcal{N}(v)|=D+1

(233)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(u)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}_{u\setminus\!\!\setminus v}\cup\mathcal{N}_{uv}\cup\{v\},

(234)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(v)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}_{v\setminus\!\!\setminus u}\cup\mathcal{N}_{uv}\cup\{u\},

(235)

which implies:

\displaystyle D+1

\displaystyle=|\mathcal{N}(u)|=\left|\mathcal{N}_{u\setminus\!\!\setminus v}\right|+\left|\mathcal{N}_{uv}\right|+1,

(236)

\displaystyle D+1

\displaystyle=|\mathcal{N}(v)|=\left|\mathcal{N}_{v\setminus\!\!\setminus u}\right|+\left|\mathcal{N}_{uv}\right|+1.

(237)

Hence, we get:

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{N}_{u\setminus\!\!\setminus v}\right|+\left|\mathcal{N}_{uv}\right|=\left|\mathcal{N}_{v\setminus\!\!\setminus u}\right|+\left|\mathcal{N}_{uv}\right|=D,

(238)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{N}_{u\setminus\!\!\setminus v}\right|=\left|\mathcal{N}_{v\setminus\!\!\setminus u}\right|,\left|\mathcal{N}_{uv}\right|\leq D.

(239)

Setting
\gamma=\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}
and using
\left|\mathcal{N}_{u\setminus\!\!\setminus v}\right|=\left|\mathcal{N}_{v\setminus\!\!\setminus u}\right|
,
T_{3}
can be written as follows:

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sin^{2}(2\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2J\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}\right)\right]\right)^{2|N_{u\setminus\!\!\setminus v}|}

(240)

\displaystyle\times\Bigg\{\underset{h,h^{\prime}\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2(h+h^{\prime})\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}\right)\right]\left(\underset{J,J^{\prime}\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2(J+J^{\prime})\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}\right)\right]\right)^{|\mathcal{N}_{uv}|}

\displaystyle-\underset{h,h^{\prime}\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2(h-h^{\prime})\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}\right)\right]\left(\underset{J,J^{\prime}\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2(J-J^{\prime})\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}\right)\right]\right)^{|\mathcal{N}_{uv}|}\Bigg\}.

For some
a,b\geq 0
and
0\leq\lambda,\mu\leq 1
, we let

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}_{u\setminus\!\!\setminus v}|

\displaystyle=aD^{\lambda},

(241)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}_{uv}|

\displaystyle=bD^{\mu}.

(242)

such that

\left|\mathcal{N}_{u\setminus\!\!\setminus v}\right|+\left|\mathcal{N}_{uv}\right|=D\Rightarrow aD^{\lambda}+bD^{\mu}=D.

(243)

Using the above,
T_{3}
can be rewritten as follows:

6

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{4\alpha^{2}}{D}\left[\underset{h\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[h^{2}\right]+(\underset{h\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}[h])^{2}\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right)\right)\cdot 1

(255)

\displaystyle\quad-\left(1-\frac{4\alpha^{2}}{D}\left[\underset{h\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[h^{2}\right]-(\underset{h\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}[h])^{2}\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right)\right)\cdot 1

\displaystyle=-\frac{8\alpha^{2}}{D}\left(\underset{h\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}[h]\right)^{2}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right).

b>0

6

\displaystyle=\underset{h,h^{\prime}\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2(h+h^{\prime})\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}\right)\right]\left(\underset{J,J^{\prime}\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2(J+J^{\prime})\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}\right)\right]\right)^{bD^{\mu}}

(257)

\displaystyle\quad-\underset{h,h^{\prime}\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2(h-h^{\prime})\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}\right)\right]\left(\underset{J,J^{\prime}\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\cos\left(2(J-J^{\prime})\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}\right)\right]\right)^{bD^{\mu}}

(258)

\displaystyle=\left[1+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-1}\right)\right]\times\begin{cases}e^{-4\alpha^{2}b\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]+\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}\right)}\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-1}\right)\right)&\text{if }\mu=1\\

1-4\alpha^{2}b\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]-\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}\right)D^{\mu-1}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{2\mu-2}\right)&\text{if }0\leq\mu<1.\end{cases}

6

6

\displaystyle=e^{-4\alpha^{2}b\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]+\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}\right)}\hskip-5.69046pt\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-1}\right)\right)^{2}\hskip-5.69046pt-e^{-4\alpha^{2}b\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]-\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}\right)}\hskip-5.69046pt\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-1}\right)\right)^{2}

(262)

\displaystyle=e^{-4\alpha^{2}b\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}\left[e^{-4\alpha^{2}b\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}}-e^{4\alpha^{2}b\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}}\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-1}\right)

(263)

\displaystyle=-2e^{-4\alpha^{2}b\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}\sinh\left(4\alpha^{2}b\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-1}\right),

(264)

\sinh x=\frac{1}{2}\left(e^{x}-e^{-x}\right)

6

\displaystyle=\left[1+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-1}\right)\right]\left[1-4\alpha^{2}b\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]+\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}\right)D^{\mu-1}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{2\mu-2}\right)\right]

(265)

\displaystyle\quad-\left[1+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-1}\right)\right]\left[1-4\alpha^{2}b\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]-\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}\right)D^{\mu-1}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{2\mu-2}\right)\right]

(266)

\displaystyle=1-4\alpha^{2}b\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]+\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}\right)D^{\mu-1}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-1}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{2\mu-2}\right)

(267)

\displaystyle\quad-1-4\alpha^{2}b\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]-\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}\right)D^{\mu-1}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-1}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{2\mu-2}\right)

(268)

\displaystyle=-8\alpha^{2}b\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{\max(-1,2\mu-2)}\right).

In summary,
6
can be expressed as follows:

-2e^{-4\alpha^{2}b\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}\sinh\left(4\alpha^{2}b\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-1}\right)&\text{if }b>0,\,\mu=1,\\
-8\alpha^{2}b\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{\max(-1,2\mu-2)}\right)&\text{if }b>0,\,0\leq\mu<1.\end{cases}

(270)

Having computed all the terms in
T_{3}
, we now begin to evaluate the possible expressions for
T_{3}
for different possible values of
a
,
b
,
\lambda
, and
\mu
.

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sin^{2}(2\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}[J]\times\begin{cases}1&\text{if }a=0,\\[7.11317pt]
e^{-4a\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-1}\right)\right)&\text{if }a>0,\,\lambda=1,\\[7.11317pt]
1-4a\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]D^{\lambda-1}\mathcal{O}\left(D^{2\lambda-2}\right)&\text{if }a>0,\,0\geq\lambda<1,\end{cases}

(271)

\displaystyle\times\begin{cases}-\frac{8\alpha^{2}}{D}\left(\underset{h\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}[h]\right)^{2}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D^{2}}\right)&\text{if }b=0,\\
-2e^{-4\alpha^{2}b\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}\sinh\left(4\alpha^{2}b\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-1}\right)&\text{if }b>0,\,\mu=1,\\
-8\alpha^{2}b\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{\max(-1,2\mu-2)}\right)&\text{if }b>0,\,0\leq\mu<1.\end{cases}

Recall that
aD^{\lambda}+bD^{\mu}=D
. We can divide into four cases, the possible values of
a
,
b
,
\lambda
, and
\mu
.

(C1)

a=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad D=bD^{\mu}\quad\Rightarrow\quad b=\mu=1

(C2)

b=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad D=aD^{\lambda}\quad\Rightarrow\quad a=\lambda=1

(C3)

\lambda<1\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mu=1
(otherwise
aD^{\lambda}+bD^{\mu}<D
for some
D
)

(C4)

\mu=1\quad\Rightarrow\quad\lambda=1
(otherwise
aD^{\lambda}+bD^{\mu}<D
for some
D
)

The above gives rise to the following cases:

[formula]
[formula]

a=0

a>0,\>\lambda=1

a>0,\>0\leq\lambda<1

b=0

Contradicts (C1)

\because a=0\Rightarrow b=1

Case 1

b=0,\;a=\lambda=1

Contradicts (C2)

\because b=0\Rightarrow\lambda=1

b>0,\;\mu=1

Case 2

a=0,\;b=\mu=1

Case 3

a,b>0,\;a+b=1,\;\lambda=\mu=1

Case 4

a,b>0,\;\mu=1,\;0\leq\lambda<1

b>0,\;0\leq\mu<1

Contradicts (C1)

\because a=0\Rightarrow\mu=1

Case 5

a,b>0,\;\lambda=1,\;0\leq\mu<1

Contradicts (C3)

\because\lambda<1\Rightarrow\mu=1

As indicated above, four of the above cases result in contradictions. We will consider the remaining 5 cases separately.

(272)

\displaystyle=4\alpha^{2}\sin^{2}(2\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]e^{-4\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}\left(\underset{h\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[h\right]\right)^{2}\frac{1}{D}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-2}\right).
