\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sin^{2}(2\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]e^{-4a\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-1}\right)\right)

(282)

\displaystyle\quad\times\left[-8b\alpha^{2}\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}D^{\mu-1}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{\max(-1,2\mu-2)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=4b\alpha^{2}\sin^{2}(2\beta)\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{3}e^{-4a\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}D^{\mu-1}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{\max(-1,2\mu-2)}\right).

(283)

Step 4: Computing the Sum of Terms
T_{2}
and
T_{3}

Recall that
T_{2}
is computed as follows:

T_{2}=\sin(4\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]\frac{2\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}e^{-2\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-1}\right).

(284)

•

Case 1:

b=0,\quad a=\lambda=1

\displaystyle T_{2}+T_{3}

\displaystyle=\sin(4\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]\frac{2\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}e^{-2\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-1}\right)

(285)

\displaystyle\quad+4\alpha^{2}\sin^{2}(2\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]e^{-4\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}\left(\underset{h\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[h\right]\right)^{2}\frac{1}{D}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sin(4\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]\frac{2\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}e^{-2\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-1}\right).

(286)

•

Case 2:

a=0,\quad b=\mu=1

\displaystyle T_{2}+T_{3}

\displaystyle=\sin(4\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]\frac{2\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}e^{-2\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}

(287)

\displaystyle+\sin^{2}(2\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]e^{-4\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}\sinh\left(4\alpha^{2}\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-1}\right).

•

Case 3:

a,b>0,\quad\lambda=\mu=1
, i.e.
a+b=1

\displaystyle T_{2}+T_{3}

\displaystyle=\sin(4\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]\frac{2\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}e^{-2\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}

(288)

\displaystyle+\sin^{2}(2\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]e^{-4\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}\sinh\left(4b\alpha^{2}\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-1}\right).

•

Case 4:

a,b>0,\quad\mu=1,\quad 0\leq\lambda<1

\displaystyle T_{2}+T_{3}

\displaystyle=\sin(4\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]\frac{2\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}e^{-2\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}+\sin^{2}(2\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]e^{-4b\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}\sinh\left(4b\alpha^{2}\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}\right)

(289)

\displaystyle-4a\alpha^{2}\sin^{2}(2\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]e^{-4b\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}\sinh\left(4b\alpha^{2}\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}\right)D^{\lambda-1}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{\max(2\lambda-2,-1)}\right).

Case 4a:

\lambda<\frac{1}{2}

Note that
\lambda<\frac{1}{2}\Leftrightarrow-\frac{1}{2}>\lambda-1
. Hence,
T_{2}+T_{3}
can be further simplified as follows:

\displaystyle T_{2}+T_{3}

\displaystyle=\sin(4\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]\frac{2\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}e^{-2\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}

(290)

\displaystyle+\sin^{2}(2\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]e^{-4b\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}\sinh\left(4b\alpha^{2}\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{\lambda-1}\right).

Case 4b:

\lambda=\frac{1}{2}

Note that
\lambda=\frac{1}{2}\Leftrightarrow-\frac{1}{2}=\lambda-1
. Hence,
T_{2}+T_{3}
can be further simplified as follows:

\displaystyle T_{2}+T_{3}

\displaystyle=\sin^{2}(2\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]e^{-4b\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}\sinh\left(4b\alpha^{2}\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}\right)

(291)

\displaystyle+\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]\Bigg\{2\alpha\sin(4\beta)e^{-2\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}

\displaystyle-4a\alpha^{2}\sin^{2}(2\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]e^{-4b\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}\sinh\left(4b\alpha^{2}\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}\right)\Bigg\}\frac{1}{\sqrt{D}}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{\max(2\lambda-2,-1)}\right).

Case 4c:

\lambda>\frac{1}{2}

Note that
\lambda>\frac{1}{2}\Leftrightarrow-\frac{1}{2}<\lambda-1
. Hence,
T_{2}+T_{3}
can be further simplified as follows:

\displaystyle T_{2}+T_{3}

\displaystyle=\sin^{2}(2\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]e^{-4b\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}\sinh\left(4b\alpha^{2}\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}\right)

(292)

\displaystyle-4a\alpha^{2}\sin^{2}(2\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]e^{-4b\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}\sinh\left(4b\alpha^{2}\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{2}\right)D^{\lambda-1}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{D}}\right).

•

Case 5:

a,b>0,\quad\lambda=1,\quad 0\leq\mu<1

\displaystyle T_{2}+T_{3}

\displaystyle=\sin(4\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]\frac{2\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}e^{-2\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-1}\right)

(293)

\displaystyle+4b\alpha^{2}\sin^{2}(2\beta)\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{3}e^{-4a\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}D^{\mu-1}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{\max(-1,2\mu-2)}\right).

Case 5a:

\mu>\frac{1}{2}

Note that
\mu>\frac{1}{2}\Rightarrow\mu-1>-\frac{1}{2}
. Now,
-\frac{1}{2}>2\mu-2\Leftrightarrow\mu<\frac{3}{4}
. Hence,
T_{2}+T_{3}
can be further simplified as follows:

T_{2}+T_{3}=4b\alpha^{2}\sin^{2}(2\beta)\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{3}e^{-4a\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}D^{\mu-1}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{\max(-\frac{1}{2},2\mu-2)}\right).

(294)

Case 5b:

\mu=\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle T_{2}+T_{3}

\displaystyle=\Bigg(2\alpha\sin(4\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]e^{-2\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}+4b\alpha^{2}\sin^{2}(2\beta)\left(\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J\right]\right)^{3}e^{-4a\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}\Bigg)\frac{1}{\sqrt{D}}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-1}\right).

(295)

Case 5c:

\mu<\frac{1}{2}

Note that
\mu<\frac{1}{2}\Rightarrow-1\leq\mu-1<-\frac{1}{2}
. Hence,
T_{2}+T_{3}
can be further simplified as follows:

T_{2}+T_{3}=\sin(4\beta)\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]\frac{2\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}e^{-2\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{\mu-1}\right).

(296)

Step 5: Computing the Scaled Expected QAOA
1
Cost Function

Recall that the expected QAOA
1
cost function for an Ising model on a graph
G=(V,E)
with parameters
\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
is given by:

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}J\sim f^{E}\\
h\sim g^{V}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\xi_{G}(\gamma,\beta)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in V}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}J\sim f^{E}\\
h\sim g^{V}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[C_{i}(\gamma,\beta;G)\right]+\hskip-11.38092pt\sum_{\{u,v\}\in E}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}J\sim f^{E}\\
h\sim g^{V}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left\{C_{uv}^{(1)}(\gamma,\beta;G)+\hskip-12.80365pt\sum_{\{u,v\}\in E}C_{uv}^{(2)}(\gamma,\beta;G)\right\}

(297)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in V}T_{1}+\sum_{\{u,v\}\in E}\left(T_{2}+T_{3}\right).

(298)

For a
(D+1)
-regular graph, the expectation terms
T_{1},T_{2}
, and
T_{3}
are identical for all vertices
i
and edges
\{u,v\}
. This allows us to simplify the expression as follows:

\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}J\sim f^{E}\\
h\sim g^{V}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\xi_{G}(\gamma,\beta)\right]=T_{1}|V|+(T_{2}+T_{3})|E|,

(299)

where
n=|V|
is the number of vertices and
|E|
is the number of edges in the graph
G
. Since the graph is
(D+1)
-regular, the number of edges satisfies
|E|=\frac{n}{2}(D+1)
. Substituting this into the equation, we obtain:

\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}J\sim f^{E}\\
h\sim g^{V}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\xi_{G}(\gamma,\beta)\right]=nT_{1}+\frac{n}{2}(D+1)(T_{2}+T_{3}).

(300)

To facilitate analysis, we introduce a scaled version of the cost function by dividing the expected cost by the number of edges
|E|
:

\frac{1}{|E|}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}J\sim f^{E}\\
h\sim g^{V}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\xi_{G}(\gamma,\beta)\right]=\frac{|V|}{|E|}T_{1}+(T_{2}+T_{3}).

(301)

Calculating the ratio
|V|/|E|
for a
(D+1)
-regular graph, we have:

\frac{|V|}{|E|}=\frac{n}{\frac{n}{2}(D+1)}=\frac{2}{D+1}.

(302)

Next, we evaluate the term
(|V|/|E|)T_{1}
to get the following:

\displaystyle\frac{|V|}{|E|}T_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{D+1}\left[\sin(4\beta)\underset{h\sim g}{\mathbb{E}}\left[h^{2}\right]\frac{2\alpha}{\sqrt{D}}e^{-2\alpha^{2}\underset{J\sim f}{\mathbb{E}}\left[J^{2}\right]}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{D}\right)\right]

(303)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-\frac{3}{2}}\right).

(304)

Finally, substituting this result into the scaled cost function yields:

\frac{1}{|E|}\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}J\sim f^{E}\\
h\sim g^{V}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\xi_{G}(\gamma,\beta)\right]=T_{2}+T_{3}+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-\frac{3}{2}}\right).

(305)

This scaling demonstrates that as the degree
D
of the graph increases, the contribution from
T_{1}
becomes negligible, and the expected scaled cost function is primarily determined by the terms
T_{2}
and
T_{3}
, with corrections of order
D^{-\frac{3}{2}}
. Therefore, the expressions derived in step 4 accurately characterise the scaled expected QAOA
1
cost function.

Step 6: Optimal
\gamma^{*}
Values for Leading-Order Terms

Before proceeding, we note that the subscripts for the expectation value are omitted, as the expressions in the remainder of this proof involve only the terms where
J\sim f
.

Not all expressions derived for the different cases yield closed-form solutions for the optimal
\alpha^{*}
value. To address this, we focus on the leading-order terms, which simplify the expressions and allow for closed-form solutions for
\alpha^{*}
. The leading-order expressions for the scaled QAOA
1
objective are given by:

\frac{1}{|E|}\mathbb{E}\left[\xi_{G}(\alpha,\beta)\right]=\begin{cases}\mathcal{C}_{1}(\alpha,\beta)+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{-1}\right),&b=0,\,a=\lambda=1,\\
\mathcal{C}_{2}(\alpha,\beta,1,1)+\mathcal{O}\left(1\right),&a=0,\,b=\mu=1,\\
\mathcal{C}_{2}(\alpha,\beta,1,b)+\mathcal{O}\left(1\right),&a,b>0,\,\lambda=\mu=1,\\
\mathcal{C}_{2}(\alpha,\beta,b,b)+\mathcal{O}\left(1\right),&a,b>0,\,\mu=1,\,\lambda<1,\\
\mathcal{C}_{3}(\alpha,\beta)+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{\mu-1}\right),&a,b>0,\,\lambda=1,\,\mu>\frac{1}{2},\\
\mathcal{C}_{1}(\alpha,\beta)+\mathcal{O}\left(D^{\mu-1}\right),&a,b>0,\,\lambda=1,\,\mu<\frac{1}{2},\end{cases}

(306)

where the functions
\mathcal{C}_{1}
,
\mathcal{C}_{2}
, and
\mathcal{C}_{3}
are defined as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{1}(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=2\alpha\sin(4\beta)\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]e^{-2\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}\frac{1}{\sqrt{D}},

(307)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{2}(\alpha,\beta,\theta_{1},\theta_{2})

\displaystyle=\sin^{2}(2\beta)\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]e^{-4\theta_{1}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}\sinh\left(4\theta_{2}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}\right),

(308)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{3}(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=4b\alpha^{2}\sin^{2}(2\beta)\mathbb{E}[J]^{3}e^{-4a\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}[J^{2}]}D^{\mu-1}.

(309)

For the case
(a,b>0,\,\lambda=1,\,\mu=\frac{1}{2})
, the leading-order terms coincide with the original expression, but they do not admit a closed-form solution for
\alpha^{*}
. In contrast, for the cases
(b=0,\,a=\lambda=1)
,
(a,b>0,\,\lambda=1,\,\mu>\frac{1}{2})
, and
(a,b>0,\,\lambda=1,\,\mu<\frac{1}{2})
, the leading-order terms also coincide with the original expressions, but they do allow for closed-form solutions for
\alpha^{*}
.

We now proceed to compute the optimal closed-form values of the three functions
\mathcal{C}_{1}
,
\mathcal{C}_{2}
, and
\mathcal{C}_{3}
.

Optimising
\mathcal{C}_{1}

To determine the optimal
\alpha
that minimises
\mathcal{C}_{1}
, we fix
\beta=3\pi/8
, simplifying
\mathcal{C}_{1}
to:

\mathcal{C}_{1}(\alpha)=-2\alpha\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]e^{-2\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}\frac{1}{\sqrt{D}}.

(310)

The optimal
\alpha
is found by differentiating
\mathcal{C}_{1}
with respect to
\alpha
and setting the derivative to zero:

\displaystyle\frac{d\,\mathcal{C}_{1}(\alpha)}{d\alpha}

\displaystyle=-2\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]\left(e^{-2\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}-4\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]e^{-2\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}\right)\frac{1}{\sqrt{D}}

(311)

\displaystyle=-2\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]\left(1-4\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]\right)e^{-2\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}\frac{1}{\sqrt{D}}.

(312)

Setting the derivative to zero yields:

1-4\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\alpha^{2}=\frac{1}{4\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\alpha=\pm\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}}.

(313)

Since
\alpha\geq 0
, the optimal value is:

\alpha^{*}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}}.

(314)

Optimising
\mathcal{C}_{2}

For
\mathcal{C}_{2}
, we set
\beta=\pi/4
, reducing the expression to:

\mathcal{C}_{2}(\alpha,\theta_{1},\theta_{2})=\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]e^{-4\theta_{1}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}\sinh\left(4\theta_{2}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}\right).

(315)

To facilitate differentiation, we take the natural logarithm:

\ln\left[\mathcal{C}_{2}(\alpha,\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\right]=\ln\left[\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]\right]-4\theta_{1}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]+\ln\left[\sinh\left(4\theta_{2}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}\right)\right].

(316)

Differentiating with respect to
\alpha
gives:

\displaystyle\frac{d\,\ln\left[\mathcal{C}_{2}(\alpha,\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\right]}{d\alpha}

\displaystyle=-8\theta_{1}\alpha\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]+8\theta_{2}\alpha\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}\coth\left(4\theta_{2}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}\right).

(317)

Setting this derivative to zero results in:

-8\theta_{1}\alpha\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]+8\theta_{2}\alpha\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}\coth\left(4\theta_{2}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}\right)=0.

(318)

It is evident that
\alpha=0
is a critical point. To identify other critical points, we divide through by
8\alpha
, assuming
\alpha\neq 0
, and solve for
\alpha^{2}
:

\displaystyle-\theta_{1}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]+\theta_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}\coth\left(4\theta_{2}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=0

(319)

\displaystyle\coth\left(4\theta_{2}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}\right)-\dfrac{\theta_{1}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}{\theta_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}}

\displaystyle=0

(320)

\displaystyle 4\theta_{2}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=\coth^{-1}\left(\dfrac{\theta_{1}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}{\theta_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}}\right)

(321)

\displaystyle 4\theta_{2}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{\frac{\theta_{1}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}{\theta_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}}+1}{\frac{\theta_{1}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}{\theta_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}}-1}\right)

(322)

\displaystyle 4\theta_{2}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{\theta_{1}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]+\theta_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}}{\theta_{1}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]-\theta_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}}\right)

(323)

\displaystyle\alpha^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\theta_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}}\ln\left(\frac{\theta_{1}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]+\theta_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}}{\theta_{1}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]-\theta_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}}\right).

(324)

As
\alpha\geq 0
, the optimal value for
\alpha>0
is:

\alpha^{*}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{8\theta_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}}\ln\left(\frac{\theta_{1}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]+\theta_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}}{\theta_{1}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]-\theta_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}}\right)}.

(325)

Optimising
\mathcal{C}_{3}

For
\mathcal{C}_{3}
, we set
\beta=\pi/4
, reducing the expression to:

\mathcal{C}_{3}(\alpha)=4b\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}[J]^{3}e^{-4a\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}[J^{2}]}D^{\mu-1}.

(326)

Differentiating with respect to
\alpha
gives:

\displaystyle\frac{d\,\mathcal{C}_{3}(\alpha)}{d\alpha}

\displaystyle=4b\mathbb{E}[J]^{3}\left[2\alpha-4a\alpha^{3}\mathbb{E}[J^{2}]\right]e^{-4a\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}[J^{2}]}D^{\mu-1}.

(327)

Setting the derivative to zero yields:

2\alpha-4a\alpha^{3}\mathbb{E}[J^{2}]=0.

(328)

It is evident that
\alpha=0
is a critical point. To identify other critical points, we divide through by
2\alpha
, assuming
\alpha\neq 0
, and solve for
\alpha
:

\displaystyle 1-2a\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}[J^{2}]

\displaystyle=0

(329)

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{2a\mathbb{E}[J^{2}]}}.

(330)

As
\alpha\geq 0
, the optimal value for
\alpha>0
is:

\alpha=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2a\mathbb{E}[J^{2}]}}.

(331)

Optimal
\alpha^{*}
Values

The closed-form expressions for the optimal
\alpha^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
values for the different cases are given by:

\alpha^{*}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}},&b=0,\,a=\lambda=1,\\
\sqrt{\frac{1}{8\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}}\ln\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}}\right)},&a=0,\,b=\mu=1,\\
\sqrt{\frac{1}{8b\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}}\ln\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]+b\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]-b\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}}\right)},&a,b>0,\,\lambda=\mu=1,\\
\sqrt{\frac{1}{8b\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}}\ln\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[J\right]^{2}}\right)},&a,b>0,\,\mu=1,\,\lambda<1,\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2a\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}},&a,b>0,\,\lambda=1,\,\mu>\frac{1}{2},\\
\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}},&a,b>0,\,\lambda=1,\,\mu<\frac{1}{2}.\end{cases}

(332)

Using the relation
\gamma=\alpha/\sqrt{D}
, the closed-form expressions for the optimal
\gamma^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
values can be readily obtained.

Step 7: Globally Optimal
\alpha^{*}
Coincides with First Local Optimum

In this final part of the proof, we demonstrate that the derived globally optimal value
\alpha^{*}
coincides with the first local optimum of the functions
\mathcal{C}_{1}
,
\mathcal{C}_{2}
, and
\mathcal{C}_{3}
.

Demonstrating Global Optimum Coincides with First Local Optimum for
\mathcal{C}_{1}

We want to show that the globally optimal parameter
eq.

314
for
\mathcal{C}_{1}
coincides with the first local optimum. Recall that the derivative of
\mathcal{C}_{1}
is given by:

\frac{d\,\mathcal{C}_{1}(\alpha)}{d\alpha}=-2\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]\left(1-4\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]\right)e^{-2\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}\frac{1}{\sqrt{D}}.

(333)

We begin by analysing the behaviour of
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime}(\alpha)
at
\alpha=0
. Substituting
\alpha=0
into the derivative, we find that

\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime}(0)=-2\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]\frac{1}{\sqrt{D}}<0,

(334)

which implies that
\mathcal{C}_{1}(\alpha)
is decreasing at
\alpha=0
. Next, we consider the interval
\alpha\in\left(0,\alpha^{*}\right)
. In this range, the term
1-4\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{J}}\left[J^{2}\right]
remains positive because
\alpha^{2}<\left(\alpha^{*}\right)^{2}
. Consequently, the derivative satisfies:

-2\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]\left(1-4\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]\right)e^{-2\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}\frac{1}{\sqrt{D}}<0.

(335)

This implies that
\mathcal{C}_{1}(\alpha)
continues to decrease throughout the interval
\left(0,\alpha^{*}\right)
. At
\alpha=\alpha^{*}
, the term
1-4\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{J}}\left[J^{2}\right]
becomes zero and so
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime}\left(\alpha^{*}\right)
evaluates to
0
thus confirming that
\alpha^{*}
is a stationary point.

Finally, we examine the behaviour of
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime}(\alpha)
for
\alpha>\alpha^{*}
. In this region, the term
1-4\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{J}}\left[J^{2}\right]
becomes negative because
\alpha^{2}>\left(\alpha^{*}\right)^{2}
. As a result, the derivative satisfies:

-2\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]\left(1-4\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]\right)e^{-2\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}\frac{1}{\sqrt{D}}>0.

(336)

This indicates that
\mathcal{C}_{1}(\alpha)
is increasing for
\alpha>\alpha^{*}
.

From this analysis, we observe that
\mathcal{C}_{1}(\alpha)
decreases for
\alpha\in\left[0,\alpha^{*}\right)
, reaches a stationary point at
\alpha=\alpha^{*}
, and increases for
\alpha>\alpha^{*}
. This behaviour establishes that
\alpha^{*}
is the first local minimum of
\mathcal{C}_{1}(\alpha)
for
\alpha\geq 0
.

Demonstrating Global Optimum Coincides with First Local Optimum for
\mathcal{C}_{2}

We want to show that the globally optimal parameter
eq.

325
for
\mathcal{C}_{2}
coincides with the first local optimum. Recall that the derivative of
\mathcal{C}_{2}
with respect to
\alpha
is:

\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\prime}(\alpha,\theta_{1},\theta_{2})=8\alpha\mathbb{E}[J]e^{-4\theta_{1}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}\left[\theta_{2}\mathbb{E}[J]^{2}\cosh\left(4\theta_{2}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}[J]^{2}\right)-\theta_{1}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]\sinh\left(4\theta_{2}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}[J]^{2}\right)\right],

(337)

where
(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
can be
(1,1)
,
(1,b)
, or
(b,b)
with
0\leq b\leq 1
. Additionally, since the variance is non-negative, we have
\mathbb{E}[J^{2}]\geq\mathbb{E}[J]^{2}
.

The case
\mathbb{E}[J]>0
is trivial because the global optimum that minimises
\mathcal{C}_{2}
is
\alpha^{*}=0
which clearly is the first local optimum. Thus, we focus on
\mathbb{E}[J]<0
. Consider
\alpha\in(0,\alpha^{*})
. Since
\alpha^{2}<(\alpha^{*})^{2}
, we can approximate the hyperbolic functions for small arguments:

\cosh(x)\approx 1+\frac{x^{2}}{2},\quad\sinh(x)\approx x+\frac{x^{3}}{6}.

(338)

Applying these approximations, the derivative becomes:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\approx 8\alpha\mathbb{E}[J]e^{-4\theta_{1}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}\left[\theta_{2}\mathbb{E}[J]^{2}\left(1+\frac{\left(4\theta_{2}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}[J]^{2}\right)^{2}}{2}\right)-\theta_{1}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]\left(4\theta_{2}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}[J]^{2}+\frac{\left(4\theta_{2}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}[J]^{2}\right)^{3}}{6}\right)\right]

(339)

\displaystyle\approx 8\alpha\mathbb{E}[J]e^{-4\theta_{1}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}\left[\theta_{2}\mathbb{E}[J]^{2}-4\theta_{1}\theta_{2}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}[J]^{2}\right]

(340)

\displaystyle=8\alpha\theta_{2}\mathbb{E}[J]^{3}e^{-4\theta_{1}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}\left[1-4\theta_{2}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]\right].

(341)

Since
\alpha^{2}<(\alpha^{*})^{2}
, the term
1-4\theta_{2}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}[J^{2}]
remains positive. Given that
\mathbb{E}[J]<0
and
\theta_{2}\geq 0
, the derivative
\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\prime}<0
. This implies that
\mathcal{C}_{2}
is decreasing on the interval
(0,\alpha^{*})
.

For
\alpha>\alpha^{*}
, assuming
\alpha
is still small enough for the above approximations to hold, the term
1-4\theta_{2}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}[J^{2}]
becomes negative because
\alpha^{2}>(\alpha^{*})^{2}
. Consequently,
\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\prime}>0
, indicating that
\mathcal{C}_{2}
is increasing for
\alpha>\alpha^{*}
.

For sufficiently large
\alpha\gg\alpha^{*}
, we approximate the hyperbolic functions as:

\cosh(x)\approx\sinh(x)\approx\frac{e^{x}}{2}.

(342)

Substituting these into the derivative gives:

\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\prime}\approx 4\alpha\mathbb{E}[J]e^{-4\theta_{1}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]}e^{4\theta_{2}\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}[J]^{2}}\left(\theta_{2}\mathbb{E}[J]^{2}-\theta_{1}\mathbb{E}\left[J^{2}\right]\right).

(343)

Given that
\theta_{2}\leq\theta_{1}
and
\mathbb{E}[J^{2}]\geq\mathbb{E}[J]^{2}
, we analyse the expression
\theta_{2}\mathbb{E}[J]^{2}-\theta_{1}\mathbb{E}[J^{2}]
:

•

If
\theta_{1}\mathbb{E}[J^{2}]>\theta_{2}\mathbb{E}[J]^{2}
, then
\theta_{2}\mathbb{E}[J]^{2}-\theta_{1}\mathbb{E}[J^{2}]<0
.

•

If
\theta_{1}\mathbb{E}[J^{2}]=\theta_{2}\mathbb{E}[J]^{2}
, then
\theta_{2}\mathbb{E}[J]^{2}-\theta_{1}\mathbb{E}[J^{2}]=0
.

Thus, for
\alpha\gg\alpha^{*}
, the derivative
\mathcal{C}_{2}^{\prime}>0
, confirming that
\mathcal{C}_{2}
continues to increase.

The analysis shows that
\mathcal{C}_{2}(\alpha)
is decreasing for
\alpha\in(0,\alpha^{*})
, attains a stationary point at
\alpha=\alpha^{*}
, and increases for
\alpha>\alpha^{*}
. This behaviour confirms that
\alpha^{*}
is the first local minimum of
\mathcal{C}_{2}(\alpha)
for
\alpha\geq 0
. Therefore, the globally optimal parameter
eq.

325
coincides with this first local optimum.

Demonstrating Global Optimum Coincides with First Local Optimum for
\mathcal{C}_{3}

Since the proof that the globally optimal value
\alpha^{*}
, defined in
eq.

331
, coincides with the first local optimum closely parallels the argument presented for
\mathcal{C}_{1}
, we omit it here for the sake of brevity.
∎
